Dropping the other nonnegative contributions proves the first assertion. For the last assertion, a character with original exact support $C$ retains exact support $C\setminus B$ after specialization. The two cuts therefore arise, before specialization, from the complete-support subfamilies $$\{C\in\mathcal F:q_{C\setminus B}\le H\},
 \qquad
 \{C\in\mathcal F:q_{C\setminus B}>H\}.$$ Apply Proposition <a href="#prop:lift-moments" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:lift-moments">11</a> to these subfamilies and then use the fiber bound. Combining coefficients can cancel frequencies but cannot create any outside the indicated ranges. ◻

</details>

In particular, specialize the same set $B$ of coordinates in every slot, with possibly different values in different slots. Applying <a href="#eq:large-denominator" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:large-denominator">[eq:large-denominator]</a> to the product law on the remaining primes incurs total specialization factor $$q_B^{1/2}\left(q_B^{1/(2(d-1))}\right)^{d-1}=q_B.$$ Two specialized $L^2$ norms incur the same factor. All moment orders needed here are fixed after $h$ is fixed. Finite products of their subpower bounds remain subpower; none of these constants depends on $l$, the specialized residues, or the retained supports.

<a id="sec:primes"></a>
### Prime estimates

The kernel construction requires two uniform estimates: an asymptotic formula for primes in residue classes, and cancellation in polynomial exponential sums over primes. We derive the first from a zero-free half-plane theorem and prove the second by Vaughan’s identity and Weyl differencing. The latter argument also records why the product cutoff and the arithmetic coefficients do not obstruct mixed differencing.

For positive integers $n$, put $$\Lambda'(n)=(\log n)\mathbf 1_{\{n\text{ is prime}\}},\qquad
 \Lambda(n)=
 \begin{cases}\log p,&n=p^j\text{ for a prime }p\text{ and }j\ge1,\\
 0,&\text{otherwise}.
 \end{cases}$$ All sums below run over integers, including sums whose endpoints are real. The constants in this section are independent of the lower coefficients of the polynomial phases.

<a id="pm-h-21"></a>
#### Primes in arithmetic progressions

Here is the precise analytic input from the companion zero-free theorem. Only its assertion about Dirichlet $L$-functions is needed.

<a id="stmt-thm-zero-free-input"></a>
<div id="thm:zero-free-input" class="theorem">

**Theorem 13** (Zero-free input, (OpenAI 2026c, Theorem 1.1) <a id="pm-cite-33"></a>[[ZeroFree](#ref-ZeroFree)]). *For every integer $b\ge1$ and every Dirichlet character $\chi$ modulo $b$, the Dirichlet $L$-function $L(s,\chi)$ has no zero in $\Re s>7/8$. This includes imprimitive characters and the trivial character of modulus $1$, whose $L$-function is $\zeta(s)$. A pole at $s=1$ for a principal character is allowed.*

</div>

<a id="stmt-prop-prime-progressions"></a>
<div id="prop:prime-progressions" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:prime-progression"></a>
**Proposition 14** (Uniform distribution in residue classes). *Uniformly for $Y\ge2$, $0\le x\le Y$, integers $b\ge1$, and reduced residue classes $c$ modulo $b$, $$\label{eq:prime-progression}
 \sum_{\substack{n\le x\\n\equiv c\pmod b}}\Lambda'(n)
 =\frac{x}{\varphi(b)}
   +O\bigl(Y^{31/32}\log^2(2bY)\bigr).$$ For a nonreduced class modulo $b>1$, the sum is $O(\log b)$ and has zero main term.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-prime-progressions"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-prime-progressions</summary>

<a id="eq:log-derivative"></a>
*Proof.* Set $\sigma_0=15/16$. We first prove the logarithmic derivative estimate $$\label{eq:log-derivative}
 \frac{L'}{L}(s,\chi)\ll\log\bigl(2b(2+|\Im s|)\bigr)$$ on $\Re s=\sigma_0$, and throughout $\sigma_0\le\Re s\le2$ when $|\Im s|\ge1$. The constants are absolute, including for imprimitive characters.

Write $\delta_\chi=1$ for a principal character and $0$ otherwise, and let $$G_\chi(s)=(s-1)^{\delta_\chi}L(s,\chi).$$ Fix radii $2-\sigma_0<r<R<2-7/8$ and use disks of radius $R$ centered at $s_t=2+it$. Theorem <a href="#thm:zero-free-input" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:zero-free-input">13</a> makes $G_\chi$ holomorphic and nonvanishing on each disk, with the principal pole removed. To bound its size there, use periodicity to write $$\sum_{n\le u}\chi(n)=\overline\chi\,u+E_\chi(u),\qquad
 |E_\chi(u)|\le2b,\qquad
 \overline\chi=\begin{cases}\varphi(b)/b,&\delta_\chi=1,\\0,&\delta_\chi=0.
 \end{cases}$$ Partial summation continues the Dirichlet series to $\Re s>0$ as $$L(s,\chi)=\overline\chi\,\frac{s}{s-1}
       +s\int_1^\infty E_\chi(u)u^{-s-1}\,du.$$ Consequently, on the disk, $|G_\chi(s)|\le C[2b(2+|t|)]^C$. At its center the absolutely convergent Euler product gives $|L(2+it,\chi)|\ge\zeta(4)/\zeta(2)$; hence $|G_\chi(s_t)|$ is bounded below by the same positive constant. Choose a holomorphic logarithm and subtract its value at $s_t$. Its real part is bounded above by $C\log(2b(2+|t|))$. Borel–Carathéodory on an intermediate disk, followed by Cauchy’s estimate on the disk of radius $r$, now gives $$\frac{G_\chi'}{G_\chi}(s)
 \ll\log(2b(2+|t|))\qquad (|s-s_t|\le r).$$ Subtracting $\delta_\chi/(s-1)$ proves <a href="#eq:log-derivative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:log-derivative">[eq:log-derivative]</a> in the asserted ranges.

We next pass through a smoothed sum so that the contour integral is absolutely convergent. Mellin inversion for $(1-u)_+$ gives, for $x\ge1$, <a id="eq:smoothed-character-primes"></a>$$\label{eq:smoothed-character-primes}
 \sum_{n\le x}(x-n)\Lambda(n)\chi(n)
 =\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{(2)}
   -\frac{L'}{L}(s,\chi)\frac{x^{s+1}}{s(s+1)}\,ds.$$ Here the interchange with the Dirichlet series is absolute. Move the line to $\Re s=\sigma_0$. Estimate <a href="#eq:log-derivative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:log-derivative">[eq:log-derivative]</a> bounds the horizontal integrals by a constant times $x^3\log(2b(2+T))/T^2$, which tends to zero with the height $T$. The sole crossed pole is at $s=1$ for the principal character; its residue is $x^2/2$. On the new vertical line, integration of $\log(2b(2+|t|))/(1+t^2)$ gives <a id="eq:smoothed-character-result"></a>$$\label{eq:smoothed-character-result}
 \sum_{n\le x}(x-n)\Lambda(n)\chi(n)
 =\delta_\chi\frac{x^2}{2}
      +O\bigl(x^{1+\sigma_0}\log(2b)\bigr).$$

For $(c,b)=1$, character orthogonality yields $$A_c(x):=\sum_{\substack{n\le x\\n\equiv c\pmod b}}
                 (x-n)\Lambda(n)
 =\frac{x^2}{2\varphi(b)}
      +O\bigl(x^{1+\sigma_0}\log(2b)\bigr).$$ There are $\varphi(b)$ character errors and a factor $1/\varphi(b)$, so the error is uniform in $b$. Crucially, sharp differencing is now performed on this nonnegative progression sum, rather than on a complex character sum. For $0<H<x$, positivity of $\Lambda$ gives $$\frac{A_c(x)-A_c(x-H)}{H}
 \le \sum_{\substack{n\le x\\n\equiv c\pmod b}}\Lambda(n)
 \le \frac{A_c(x+H)-A_c(x)}{H}.$$ Take $H=x^{31/32}$ for sufficiently large $x$. The main terms differ from $x/\varphi(b)$ by $O(H)$, and the errors are $$O\bigl(x^{1+\sigma_0}/H\cdot\log(2b)\bigr)
   =O\bigl(x^{31/32}\log(2b)\bigr).$$ Bounded $x$ is harmless. Finally, $$\sum_{\substack{p^j\le Y\\j\ge2}}\log p
 \ll Y^{1/2}\log^2(2Y),$$ so removing higher prime powers proves <a href="#eq:prime-progression" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:prime-progression">[eq:prime-progression]</a>. If a prime lies in a nonreduced residue class modulo $b$, it divides $b$; the sum of the logarithms of distinct such primes is at most $\log b$. This proves the last assertion. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-22"></a>
#### A minor-arc estimate over primes

<a id="stmt-prop-prime-minor-arcs"></a>
<div id="prop:prime-minor-arcs" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:prime-minor-arc"></a>
**Proposition 15** (Prime polynomial sums). *For fixed $k\ge2$ and $0<\rho<1$, there is a constant $c_m=c_m(k,\rho)\in(0,1/4)$ with the following property. If $Y$ is sufficiently large, $I\subseteq[1,Y]$ is an interval, and $f\in\mathbb R[x]$ has degree at most $k$ with coefficient $b_k$ of $x^k$, then $$\label{eq:prime-minor-arc}
 \left|\sum_{n\in I}\Lambda'(n)e(f(n))\right|
 \ll_{k,\rho}Y^{1-c_m},$$ provided $\theta=(k!)^2b_k$ has an approximation $a/q$ satisfying <a id="eq:minor-rational-range"></a>$$\label{eq:minor-rational-range}
 (a,q)=1,\qquad |\theta-a/q|\le q^{-2},\qquad
 Y^\rho\le q\le Y^{k-\rho}.$$*

</div>

Vaughan’s identity will split the prime weight into sums with one short variable and bilinear sums. Weyl differencing turns each into averages of linear phases, which rational approximation then bounds. We first prove the needed spacing and differencing estimates. Uniformity in the lower coefficients and in the interval will later permit additive linear twists for periodic restrictions and partial summation for smooth weights in the kernel construction.

Write $\|u\|$ for the distance of $u\in\mathbb R$ to the nearest integer, and interpret $\min(M,\|u\|^{-1})$ as $M$ when $\|u\|=0$.

<a id="stmt-lem-rational-spacing"></a>
<div id="lem:rational-spacing" class="lemma">

**Lemma 16** (Rational spacing). *Suppose $(a,q)=1$, $q\ge2$, and $|\theta-a/q|\le q^{-2}$. For $R\ge1$ and $M\ge1$, <a id="eq:rational-spacing"></a>$$\label{eq:rational-spacing}
 \sum_{1\le r\le R}\min(M,\|\theta r\|^{-1})
 \ll (R/q+1)\bigl(M+q\log(2q)\bigr).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-rational-spacing"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-rational-spacing</summary>

*Proof.* Within a block of $\lfloor q/2\rfloor$ consecutive integers, two distinct indices differ by a nonzero $d$ with $|d|<q/2$. Thus $$\|\theta d\|\ge \|ad/q\|-|d|/q^2\ge1/(2q).$$ The associated points are $1/(2q)$ separated on the circle. The nearest point to zero contributes at most $M$, and summing the others in bands of width $1/(2q)$ gives $O(q\log(2q))$, with another $O(M)$ for the bounded number of points in the first band. Partitioning the indices into such blocks proves the result, also when $q=2$ or $3$. ◻

</details>

We will repeatedly group a fixed number of nonzero integer factors by their product. If that product has absolute value at most $Y^{C_k}$, the number of such factorizations is $Y^{o(1)}$, uniformly in the product. Indeed, its prime factorization bounds the number by a fixed order divisor function, up to a bounded number of sign choices. For every $\epsilon>0$ that function is $O_{k,\epsilon}(Y^\epsilon)$: allocating a prime exponent among finitely many factors gives a polynomial in the exponent, which is bounded by any fixed positive power of the prime power after allowing a constant for finitely many small primes.

The following two differencing bounds isolate the treatment of coefficients and summation domains. They are forms of classical Weyl differencing; see (Weyl 1916; Vaughan 1997) <a id="pm-cite-34"></a>[[Weyl1916](#ref-Weyl1916), [Vaughan1997](#ref-Vaughan1997)] for the method.

<a id="stmt-lem-prime-differencing"></a>
<div id="lem:prime-differencing" class="lemma">

**Lemma 17** (One-variable and mixed differencing). *Let $k\ge2$, and let $f\in\mathbb R[x]$ have degree at most $k$, with coefficient $b_k$ of $x^k$.*

<a id="eq:type-one-differencing"></a>
*If $J$ is an interval of integers of length at most an integer $M\ge1$, and $m$ is a positive integer, then $$\label{eq:type-one-differencing}
 \left|\frac1M\sum_{n\in J}e(f(mn))\right|^{2^{k-1}}
 \ll_k\frac1{M^k}
 \sum_{|u_1|,\ldots,|u_{k-1}|<M}
 \min\bigl(M,\|k!b_km^ku_1\cdots u_{k-1}\|^{-1}\bigr).$$*

<a id="eq:type-two-differencing"></a>
*Next let $A,B$ be positive integers, let $I\subseteq[1,Y]$ be an interval, and let $|a_m|,|d_n|\le1$. Set $$S=\sum_{\substack{A\le m<2A\\B\le n<2B}}
       a_md_n\mathbf 1_{\{mn\in I\}}e(f(mn)),\qquad
 \theta=(k!)^2b_k.$$ Then $$\label{eq:type-two-differencing}
 \left|\frac{S}{AB}\right|^{2^{2k-1}}
 \ll_k\frac1{A^kB^k}
 \sum_{\substack{|u_1|,\ldots,|u_k|<B\\
                  |v_1|,\ldots,|v_{k-1}|<A}}
 \min\bigl(A,\|\theta u_1\cdots u_kv_1\cdots v_{k-1}\|^{-1}\bigr).$$ Zero shifts are included in both sums.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-prime-differencing"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-prime-differencing</summary>

*Proof.* We give the mixed argument explicitly. For a function $g$ of two integer variables, define $$\Delta^n_u g(m,n)=g(m,n+u)-g(m,n),\qquad
 \Delta^m_v g(m,n)=g(m+v,n)-g(m,n).$$ Let $D_0$ be the set of pairs in the displayed box with $mn\in I$. Extend $a_m$ and $d_n$ by zero outside their respective intervals, and extend every summand by zero outside $D_0$. Cauchy–Schwarz first removes $a_m$: $$\left|\frac{S}{AB}\right|^2
 \le\frac1A\sum_{A\le m<2A}
       \left|\frac1B\sum_n d_n\mathbf 1_{D_0}(m,n)e(f(mn))\right|^2.$$ Expanding the square and grouping by $u=n'-n$ bounds its right side by $$\frac1B\sum_{|u|<B}
 \left|\frac1{AB}\sum_{m,n}
    d_{n+u}\overline{d_n}\,
    \mathbf 1_{D_0}(m,n+u)\mathbf 1_{D_0}(m,n)
    e\bigl(\Delta^n_u f(mn)\bigr)\right|.$$ For fixed $u$ the new weight depends only on $n$ and has modulus at most one. This is exactly the form needed for another $n$-difference. In particular, the product cutoff remains inside the sum; it has not been replaced by a rectangular domain.

For $j$ fixed shifts $\mathbf u=(u_1,\ldots,u_j)$, let $$\begin{split}
 D_{\mathbf u}&=\{(m,n):
      (m,n+\varepsilon\cdot\mathbf u)\in D_0
                   \text{ for every }\varepsilon\in\{0,1\}^j\},\\
 F_{\mathbf u}(m,n)&=
    \Delta^n_{u_j}\cdots\Delta^n_{u_1}f(mn).
 \end{split}$$ The accompanying coefficient $d_{\mathbf u}(n)$ is defined recursively by $d_\varnothing(n)=d_n$ and $$d_{(\mathbf u,u)}(n)
   =d_{\mathbf u}(n+u)\overline{d_{\mathbf u}(n)}.$$ It depends only on $n$ and is bounded by one. At each subsequent step, Cauchy–Schwarz in the existing shifts costs only a constant depending on $j$, because their total normalized mass is at most $2^j$. Cauchy–Schwarz in $m$, followed by the same square expansion, adds one $n$-shift. Induction therefore gives <a id="eq:n-difference-stage"></a>$$\label{eq:n-difference-stage}
 \left|\frac{S}{AB}\right|^{2^k}
 \ll_k\frac1{B^k}\sum_{|u_1|,\ldots,|u_k|<B}
 \left|\frac1{AB}\sum_{m,n}
       d_{\mathbf u}(n)\mathbf 1_{D_{\mathbf u}}(m,n)
       e(F_{\mathbf u}(m,n))\right|.$$ All summands still have $m$ and $n$ in their original intervals, as the zero vertex of every translated cube belongs to $D_0$.

After $k$ differences in $n$, every lower-degree term vanishes, and $$F_{\mathbf u}(m,n)=k!b_k u_1\cdots u_k m^k.$$ For $0\le j\le k-1$ and $m$-shifts $\mathbf v=(v_1,\ldots,v_j)$, define the domains $$\begin{split}
 D_{\mathbf u,\mathbf v}=\{(m,n):\;&
 (m+\eta\cdot\mathbf v,n+\varepsilon\cdot\mathbf u)\in D_0\\
 &\text{for every }\eta\in\{0,1\}^j,
                         \ \varepsilon\in\{0,1\}^k\}.
 \end{split}$$ Thus $D_{\mathbf u,\varnothing}=D_{\mathbf u}$. For $1\le j\le k-1$, let $$T_{\mathbf u,\mathbf v}
 =\frac1{AB}\sum_{m,n}\mathbf 1_{D_{\mathbf u,\mathbf v}}(m,n)
 e\bigl(\Delta^m_{v_j}\cdots\Delta^m_{v_1}F_{\mathbf u}(m,n)\bigr).$$ To enter this unweighted family, remove $d_{\mathbf u}$ by Cauchy–Schwarz in $n$: $$\left|\frac1{AB}\sum_{m,n}
 d_{\mathbf u}(n)\mathbf 1_{D_{\mathbf u}}(m,n)e(F_{\mathbf u}(m,n))
 \right|^2
 \le\frac1B\sum_{B\le n<2B}
 \left|\frac1A\sum_m
    \mathbf 1_{D_{\mathbf u}}(m,n)e(F_{\mathbf u}(m,n))\right|^2.$$ Expanding the square bounds this by $A^{-1}\sum_{|v_1|<A}|T_{\mathbf u,(v_1)}|$. This is the first $m$-differencing step, so removing the coefficients requires no additional squaring. For $1\le j\le k-2$, Cauchy–Schwarz in $n$ and the same square expansion give $$|T_{\mathbf u,\mathbf v}|^2
 \le\frac1A\sum_{|v|<A}|T_{\mathbf u,(\mathbf v,v)}|.$$ Applying Cauchy–Schwarz also to all existing shift variables, whose total normalized mass is bounded in terms of $k$, now yields <a id="eq:m-difference-stage"></a>$$\label{eq:m-difference-stage}
 \left|\frac{S}{AB}\right|^{2^{k+j}}
 \ll_k\frac1{B^kA^j}
 \sum_{\substack{|u_1|,\ldots,|u_k|<B\\|v_1|,\ldots,|v_j|<A}}
          |T_{\mathbf u,\mathbf v}|\qquad(1\le j\le k-1).$$ This accounts for all $k+(k-1)$ squarings.

Take $j=k-1$. For fixed $n$, the section of $D_{\mathbf u,\mathbf v}$ is an interval of $m$’s, possibly empty. In fact each condition puts $m+\eta\cdot\mathbf v$ in $[A,2A)$ and its product with the positive number $n+\varepsilon\cdot\mathbf u$ in $I$; it therefore specifies an interval in $m$. Their intersection is an interval of length at most $A$. The final phase $\Delta^m_{v_{k-1}}\cdots\Delta^m_{v_1}F_{\mathbf u}$ is linear in $m$ with coefficient $$(k!)^2b_k\,u_1\cdots u_kv_1\cdots v_{k-1}.$$ A geometric-sum estimate bounds each $m$-sum by the corresponding minimum in <a href="#eq:type-two-differencing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:type-two-differencing">[eq:type-two-differencing]</a>. Summing over at most $B$ values of $n$, the remaining normalized sum is bounded by that minimum divided by $A$. The shifts have normalizing factor $B^{-k}A^{-(k-1)}$, which proves <a href="#eq:type-two-differencing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:type-two-differencing">[eq:type-two-differencing]</a>.

For <a href="#eq:type-one-differencing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:type-one-differencing">[eq:type-one-differencing]</a>, perform the same square expansion $k-1$ times in a single variable. The translated intersections of $J$ are intervals of length at most $M$, and the final linear coefficient is $k!b_km^ku_1\cdots u_{k-1}$. The normalizing factor is $M^{-(k-1)}$, followed by $M^{-1}$ for the final geometric sum. This proves the first bound as well. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-prime-differencing-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-prime-differencing</summary>

<a id="eq:vaughan-identity"></a>
*Proof of Proposition <a href="#prop:prime-minor-arcs" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:prime-minor-arcs">15</a>.* We now apply the differencing bounds to Vaughan’s decomposition. We first prove the estimate with $\Lambda$ in place of $\Lambda'$. Put $e_0=\rho/(100k)$ and $Z=Y^{e_0}$. For an arithmetic function $g$, write $g_{\le Z}(n)=g(n)\mathbf 1_{\{n\le Z\}}$ and $g_{>Z}=g-g_{\le Z}$. Let $\mu$ denote the Möbius function, let $1$ denote the constant arithmetic function, and use $*$ for Dirichlet convolution. Vaughan’s identity, in the form used here, is $$\label{eq:vaughan-identity}
 \Lambda=\Lambda_{\le Z}+\mu_{\le Z}*\log
       -\mu_{\le Z}*\Lambda_{\le Z}*1
       +\mu_{>Z}*1*\Lambda_{>Z}.$$ This decomposition is classical; see (Vaughan 1997, vol. 125, chap. 3) <a id="pm-cite-35"></a>[[Vaughan1997](#ref-Vaughan1997)]. It follows directly from $\log=1*\Lambda$ and $\mu*1=\mathbf 1_{\{1\}}$: split $\mu$ at $Z$ in $\mu*\log$, and then split $\Lambda$ at $Z$ in the term $\mu_{>Z}*1*\Lambda$.

*The two sums with a short variable.* The middle two terms of <a href="#eq:vaughan-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:vaughan-identity">[eq:vaughan-identity]</a> reduce to sums with an outer variable $m\le Z^2$ and an inner variable $n\le Y/m$. The outer coefficient is either $\mu(m)$ or $\sum_{ab=m,\,a,b\le Z}\mu(a)\Lambda(b)$, and therefore has size $Y^{o(1)}$. The inner weight is respectively $\log n$ or $1$. Fix $m$, let $M=\lceil Y/m\rceil$, and consider any interval subsum $S_m=\sum_{n\in J}e(f(mn))$ of the inner sum.

<a id="eq:type-one-saving"></a>
Apply <a href="#eq:type-one-differencing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:type-one-differencing">[eq:type-one-differencing]</a>. For every integer $r$, $$\min(M,\|k!b_kr\|^{-1})
 \le k!\min(M,\|\theta r\|^{-1}),$$ since $\|k!u\|\le k!\|u\|$. Tuples with a zero shift contribute $O_k(M^{-1})$ after normalization. For the others group by $r=m^k|u_1\cdots u_{k-1}|\le m^kM^{k-1}$ and apply Lemma <a href="#lem:rational-spacing" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:rational-spacing">16</a>. The divisor bound gives $$\label{eq:type-one-saving}
 \left|\frac{S_m}{M}\right|^{2^{k-1}}
 \ll Y^{o(1)}\left(
 \frac1M+\frac{m^k}{q}+\frac{m^k}{M}
       +\frac1{M^{k-1}}+\frac q{M^k}\right).$$ Here and below logarithmic factors are included in $Y^{o(1)}$. For completeness, the four terms after $1/M$ result from expanding $$M^{-k}\left(\frac{m^kM^{k-1}}q+1\right)(M+q\log(2q)).$$ As $m\le Y^{2e_0}$ and $M\ge Y^{1-2e_0}$, $$\frac{m^k}{q},\ \frac q{M^k}\le Y^{-\rho+2ke_0},\qquad
 \frac{m^k}{M}\le Y^{-1+2(k+1)e_0}.$$ The remaining terms also save a positive power of $Y$. Taking the $2^{k-1}$-st root in <a href="#eq:type-one-saving" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:type-one-saving">[eq:type-one-saving]</a> therefore gives $S_m\ll M Y^{-c}$ for some $c=c(k,\rho)>0$, uniformly in the subinterval. Partial summation permits the weight $\log n$ with a further $O(\log Y)$ factor. Since $$\sum_{m\le Z^2}\lceil Y/m\rceil\ll Y\log(2Y),$$ both short-variable terms save a fixed power of $Y$ after including the outer coefficients.

*The bilinear sum.* For the final term of <a href="#eq:vaughan-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:vaughan-identity">[eq:vaughan-identity]</a>, group the factors as $(\mu_{>Z}*1)(m)\Lambda_{>Z}(n)$. Each coefficient is $Y^{o(1)}$ when $mn\le Y$, and a nonzero coefficient requires both $m>Z$ and $n>Z$. Partition into dyadic boxes $$A\le m<2A,\qquad B\le n<2B,
 \qquad A,B\ge Z/2,\qquad AB\le Y.$$ There are $O(\log^2(2Y))$ relevant boxes. Divide out the coefficient bounds to reduce to $|a_m|,|d_n|\le1$. If $AB<Y^{1-e_0}$, the trivial estimate is already sufficient. Otherwise put $R=A^{k-1}B^k$ and apply <a href="#eq:type-two-differencing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:type-two-differencing">[eq:type-two-differencing]</a>.

<a id="eq:type-two-saving"></a>
There are $k$ shifts of range $B$ and $k-1$ of range $A$. Tuples containing a zero shift contribute $O_k(A^{-1}+B^{-1})$. Group the others by their nonzero absolute product, at most $R$. Lemma <a href="#lem:rational-spacing" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:rational-spacing">16</a> and the divisor bound imply $$\label{eq:type-two-saving}
 \left|\frac{S}{AB}\right|^{2^{2k-1}}
 \ll Y^{o(1)}\left(
 \frac1A+\frac1B+\frac1q+\frac1R+\frac q{AR}\right).$$ Indeed the nonzero-shift bound before expansion is $$\frac{Y^{o(1)}}{AR}(R/q+1)(A+q\log(2q));$$ the additional term $O(\log(2q)/A)$ is included in $Y^{o(1)}/A$. Now $A,B\ge Y^{e_0}/2$, while $$AR=(AB)^k\ge Y^{k(1-e_0)},\qquad
 \frac q{AR}\le Y^{-\rho+ke_0}.$$ Thus <a href="#eq:type-two-saving" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:type-two-saving">[eq:type-two-saving]</a> saves a fixed power. Restoring the coefficients and summing the boxes preserves a positive saving.

The remaining term $\Lambda_{\le Z}$ costs only $O(Z\log(2Z))$. This proves the required estimate for $\Lambda$, with some positive exponent depending only on $k,\rho$. Removing higher prime powers costs $O(Y^{1/2}\log^2(2Y))$, as in Proposition <a href="#prop:prime-progressions" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:prime-progressions">14</a>. Decreasing the exponent if necessary gives $c_m\in(0,1/4)$ and proves <a href="#eq:prime-minor-arc" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:prime-minor-arc">[eq:prime-minor-arc]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:kernel"></a>
### A kernel on differences at prime arguments

The tuple laws prescribe a distribution of admissible increments along each edge of the distinguished cycle. We now realize its low-denominator Fourier multipliers by a signed kernel supported on polynomial values at prime arguments. This will turn avoidance of those values into cancellation for a pair of Fourier lifts. The kernel comparison adapts the integer-argument method of (OpenAI 2026a) <a id="pm-cite-36"></a>[[IntersectivePower](#ref-IntersectivePower)]; the unit conditions in its local weights and their realization using prime arguments are the additional ingredients here.

<a id="pm-h-24"></a>
#### Parameters and the pair estimate

<a id="eq:parameters"></a>
<a id="eq:kernel-cutoffs"></a>
Fix the following parameters, all depending only on $h$ and the tuple construction: $$\label{eq:parameters}
\begin{split}
 &\eta=\frac{1}{10000k},\qquad
 c_m=c_m(k,\eta/4)\quad\text{from Proposition~\ref{prop:prime-minor-arcs}},
 \qquad \nu=\frac{\min(\eta,c_m/k)}{10},\\
 &\beta=\frac{\nu}{100},\qquad \zeta=\frac{\nu}{100},\qquad
 \tau=\frac{\beta\gamma}{1000k(d+1)},\qquad
 \sigma=\frac{\gamma\tau}{4}.
\end{split}$$ Here $\eta$ controls the major arcs, $\nu$ is a common saving from prime sums, $\beta$ controls the Fourier cutoffs, and $\zeta$ bounds the auxiliary parameter $l$. The smaller exponents $\tau$ and $\sigma$ are reserved for the descent in Section <a href="#sec:descent" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:descent">7</a>. The parameter $\eta$ is distinct from the exceptional masses $\eta_{l,p}$. For a scale $N$ put $$\label{eq:kernel-cutoffs}
 Q=N^\beta,\qquad H=N^{\beta/2},\qquad
 \mathcal P_N=\{p\text{ prime}:P\le p\le Q\}.$$

<a id="eq:edge-multiplier-bound"></a>
Fix $l\ge1$, write $F=h_l$, and choose a directed cycle edge $v_j\to v_{j+1}$. For each $p\ge P$, let $\pi_p$ be the distribution on $\mathbb F_p$ of $z_{v_{j+1}}-z_{v_j}$ under $\mu_{l,p}^\circ$. It is supported on admissible residues by Proposition <a href="#prop:tuple-laws" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:tuple-laws">6</a>. For $v\in\mathbb Z$ define $$\Gamma_p(v/p)=\sum_{y\in\mathbb F_p}\pi_p(y)e(vy/p).$$ For a nonzero character, the conditional mixing estimate <a href="#eq:tuple-mixing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tuple-mixing">[eq:tuple-mixing]</a>, applied to the second endpoint and paired with the conjugate character at the first, gives $|\Gamma_p(v/p)|\le p^{-\gamma}$. If $\xi$ has squarefree denominator supported on primes at least $P$, write $\xi=\sum_{p\mid q(\xi)}\xi_p$ for its prime-denominator decomposition and put $\Gamma(\xi)=\prod_{p\mid q(\xi)}\Gamma_p(\xi_p)$. Thus $$\label{eq:edge-multiplier-bound}
 \Gamma(0)=1,\qquad |\Gamma(\xi)|\le q(\xi)^{-\gamma}.$$ The multiplier may depend on $l$ and the chosen edge; all estimates below are uniform in both.

<a id="stmt-prop-pair-cancellation"></a>
<div id="prop:pair-cancellation" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:pair-cancellation"></a>
**Proposition 18** (Cancellation for an ordered pair). *Suppose $N$ is sufficiently large, $l\le N^\zeta$, and $D=M_0\prod_{p\in B}p\le N^\beta$, where $B$ is a finite set of primes at least $P$. Let $J_1,J_2:[N]\to\mathbb C$ have modulus at most one and be supported on residue classes $a'_1,a'_2\pmod D$. Assume that $r=a'_2-a'_1\pmod D$ is admissible at $l$ and that $$J_1(x)J_2(y)\ne0\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
 x<y\quad\text{and}\quad y-x\notin\mathcal D_l.$$ Set $$\Xi=\{\xi\in\mathbb Q/\mathbb Z:q(\xi)\le H,
       \ q(\xi)\text{ squarefree},\ (q(\xi),D)=1\}.$$ Then, for the multiplier of any directed cycle edge, $$\label{eq:pair-cancellation}
 D\left|\sum_{\xi\in\Xi}\Gamma(\xi)
       \widehat{J_1}_{[N]}(-\xi)\widehat{J_2}_{[N]}(\xi)\right|
 \ll H^{-\gamma/2}.$$*

</div>

<a id="eq:model-kernel"></a>
Since $M_0$ contains every prime below $P$, the multiplier is defined on all of $\Xi$. To prove the proposition, consider the model kernel $$\label{eq:model-kernel}
 k_0(n)=\frac DN\,
   \mathbf1_{\{1\le n\le N\}}\mathbf1_{\{n\equiv r\ (D)\}}
   \sum_{\xi\in\Xi}\Gamma(\xi)e(-\xi n).$$ We will construct a kernel $k_1$ supported on $\mathcal D_l$ such that $\sup_\alpha|\sum_n(k_0(n)-k_1(n))e(\alpha n)|\ll H^{-\gamma/2}$. The congruence restriction in $k_0$ will be imposed on polynomial arguments by a local weight; the factors $\Gamma_p$ will be imposed by further weights of mean one.

<a id="pm-h-25"></a>
#### Weights on polynomial arguments

<a id="eq:unit-mean-count"></a>
For a periodic function of the argument $m$, its *unit mean modulo $b$* is the uniform average over the classes $m\pmod b$ satisfying $(T_l(m),b)=1$. There are exactly $$\label{eq:unit-mean-count}
 n_b=b\prod_{\substack{p\mid b\\p\nmid l}}(1-1/p)
     =\frac{\varphi(lb)}{\varphi(l)}$$ such classes. Indeed, a prime dividing $l$ excludes no argument because $(r_l,l)=1$, and any other prime excludes exactly one class modulo that prime. These means factor over prime powers. They are also consistent under replacing $b$ by a multiple: each allowed class has the same number of allowed lifts.

At a prime $p\mid D$, choose an argument witnessing admissibility of $r\pmod {p^{E_p}}$, and denote the valuation of its derivative by $s_p$. Lemma <a href="#lem:local-lifting" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:local-lifting">4</a> supplies a class modulo $p^{E_p-s_p}$ on which the unit condition holds and $F$ is constantly $r$ modulo $p^{E_p}$. Uniform argument lifts in this class give uniform higher lifts of that value. Combining the chosen classes gives one class modulo $$d_r=\prod_{p\mid D}p^{E_p-s_p}\mid D.$$ Let $\rho_D$ be $n_{d_r}$ times its indicator. Then $\rho_D$ has unit mean one and $\|\rho_D\|_\infty\le D$.

For every prime $p\nmid D$, necessarily $p\ge P$, choose, for each $y$ with $\pi_p(y)>0$, one allowed regular argument $x_y\pmod p$ satisfying $F(x_y)=y$. Define a function $\psi_p$ modulo $p$ by $$\psi_p(x_y)=n_p\pi_p(y),\qquad
 \psi_p(x)=0\quad\text{at all other classes}.$$ It has unit mean one, and its weighted $F$-value distribution is precisely $\pi_p$. Put $$B_p(m)=\psi_p(m)-1,\qquad
 B_u(m)=\prod_{p\mid u}B_p(m),\qquad B_1=1,$$ where $u$ is squarefree and $(u,D)=1$. Each $B_p$ has unit mean zero, $B_u$ has period $u$, and $|B_u|\le u$. In particular these definitions specify the weights also on nonunit arguments; those arguments will have zero main term in the prime-progression estimate.

Expanding a finite product of $\psi_p=1+B_p$ produces a sum of the $B_u$ over squarefree products of those primes. We retain $u\le H$ to bound the periods of the weights. In a unit average against a rational phase $e(\alpha F(m))$, any prime factor of $u$ absent from $q(\alpha)$ makes that term vanish. Thus, when the part of $q(\alpha)$ supported away from $D$ is at most $H$, the truncation retains every potentially nonzero term of the corresponding product expansion. The comparison below will also bound the contribution from larger denominators.

<a id="eq:kernel-polynomial-scale"></a>
<a id="eq:prime-kernel"></a>
By Lemma <a href="#lem:auxiliary" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:auxiliary">2</a>, all coefficients of $F$ are $O_h(a_l)$. Consequently there is an integer $x_*\ge1$, independent of $l$, such that for $x\ge x_*$, $$\label{eq:kernel-polynomial-scale}
 F(x)\asymp a_lx^k,\qquad F'(x)\asymp a_lx^{k-1},\qquad
 |F''(x)|\ll a_lx^{k-2},$$ with $F,F'$ positive. Put $U=(N/a_l)^{1/k}$. Since $a_l$ is a positive integer and $a_l\le al^k$, $$U\le N^{1/k},\qquad U\gg N^{1/k-\zeta}.$$ For large $N$ there is therefore a unique $X\ge x_*$ with $F(X)=N$, and $X\asymp U$. With $\Lambda'(n)=(\log n)\mathbf1_{\{n\text{ prime}\}}$, define the signed kernel below. The factor $\varphi(l)/l$ compensates for the prime density in the progression $T_l(m)$, and $F'(m)/N$ turns the resulting $m$-integral into normalized measure on the $F$-values: $$\label{eq:prime-kernel}
\begin{split}
 k_1(n)=\sum_{x_*<m\le X}&\mathbf1_{\{n=F(m)\}}
 \frac{F'(m)}{N}\frac{\varphi(l)}{l}\Lambda'(T_l(m))\rho_D(m)\\[-2pt]
 &\hspace{15mm}\times
 \sum_{\substack{u\le H\\u\text{ squarefree}\\(u,D)=1}}B_u(m).
\end{split}$$ Here and below the argument $m$ is an integer in sums. Every nonzero term has $0<F(m)\le N$ and $T_l(m)$ prime, so $k_1$ is supported on $\mathcal D_l\cap[N]$. No positivity is required of $k_1$.

<a id="eq:prime-kernel-range"></a>
Choose a fixed constant $C$ so that $Y=ClU$ bounds $T_l(X)$. The relation $a_l=al^k/\lambda(l)$ gives $$\label{eq:prime-kernel-range}
 Y\gg N^{1/k},\qquad Y\ll N^{1/k+\zeta},\qquad
 Y^k\asymp N\lambda(l).$$ In particular $Y\le N$ for sufficiently large $N$.

<a id="pm-h-26"></a>
#### Uniform Fourier comparison

For $i=0,1$ use positive-sign transforms $S_i(\alpha)=\sum_n k_i(n)e(\alpha n)$ on $\mathbb R/\mathbb Z$. The signs differ from the negative-sign convention for Fourier coefficients of functions; this choice makes $S_0$ concentrate at the frequencies occurring in <a href="#eq:model-kernel" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:model-kernel">[eq:model-kernel]</a>.

<a id="stmt-lem-kernel-comparison"></a>
<div id="lem:kernel-comparison" class="lemma">

**Lemma 19** (Comparison of the two kernels). *For the data and ranges of Proposition <a href="#prop:pair-cancellation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:pair-cancellation">18</a>, <a id="eq:kernel-comparison"></a>$$\label{eq:kernel-comparison}
 \sup_{\alpha\in\mathbb R/\mathbb Z}|S_0(\alpha)-S_1(\alpha)|
 \ll H^{-\gamma/2}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-kernel-comparison"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-kernel-comparison</summary>

*Proof.* Use major arcs of radius $3N^{-1+\eta}$ around the reduced rationals with denominator at most $N^\eta$. They are disjoint for large $N$, since $3\eta<1$. The remaining points form the minor arcs. We first bound both transforms on the minor arcs; on a major arc we will compare their local coefficients.

<a id="eq:model-transform"></a>
<a id="eq:model-minor"></a>
Write $V_N(y)=N^{-1}\sum_{n\in[N]}e(yn)$. Expanding the congruence indicator in <a href="#eq:model-kernel" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:model-kernel">[eq:model-kernel]</a> gives $$\label{eq:model-transform}
 S_0(\alpha)=\sum_{\xi\in\Xi}\sum_{j'\bmod D}
 \Gamma(\xi)e(-j'r/D)V_N(\alpha-\xi+j'/D).$$ There are $O(DH^2)$ terms. Their centers $\xi-j'/D$ are distinct: an equality would give a rational with denominator both coprime to $D$ and dividing $D$, and hence an integer. Each center has denominator at most $DH\le N^{3\beta/2}\le N^\eta$. On minor arcs, geometric summation therefore gives $$\label{eq:model-minor}
 |S_0(\alpha)|\ll DH^2N^{-\eta}.$$

To estimate $S_1$, first consider, with any linear twist $y'$, the sums $$\sum_{m\in I}\Lambda'(T_l(m))e(\alpha F(m)+y'm)$$ over subintervals $I$ of $(x_*,X]$. On setting $n=T_l(m)$, expand the restriction $n\equiv r_l\pmod l$ as the average of its $l$ additive characters. Each resulting prime sum has a polynomial phase in $n$ whose coefficient of $n^k$ is $b_k=\alpha a/\lambda(l)$; the new twists change only the linear coefficient because $k\ge2$.

Fix a representative of $\alpha$ and apply Dirichlet approximation to $\vartheta=(k!)^2b_k$ with $$Q_0=\lfloor Y^kN^{-\eta/2}\rfloor.$$ The resulting reduced fraction $a'/q$ satisfies $q\le Q_0$ and $|\vartheta-a'/q|\le1/(qQ_0)\le q^{-2}$. By <a href="#eq:prime-kernel-range" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:prime-kernel-range">[eq:prime-kernel-range]</a>, this places $\alpha$ within $O(N^{-1+\eta/2})$ of a rational of denominator at most $(k!)^2aq$. For a minor-arc point that denominator must exceed $N^\eta$ for large $N$. Hence, using $Y\le N$ and absorbing the fixed factor $(k!)^2a$, $$Y^{\eta/4}\le q\le Y^{k-\eta/4}.$$ Proposition <a href="#prop:prime-minor-arcs" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:prime-minor-arcs">15</a> applies uniformly in the linear twists and in the interval, and gives a bound $O(Y^{1-c_m})$. The relation $Y=ClU$ and the lower bound in <a href="#eq:prime-kernel-range" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:prime-kernel-range">[eq:prime-kernel-range]</a> imply $$Y^{1-c_m}\ll U\,lN^{-c_m/k}
 \le U N^{\zeta-c_m/k}\ll U N^{-\nu}.$$ This accounts for the dependence on $l$ before any periodic weight is expanded.

<a id="eq:prime-minor-kernel"></a>
By <a href="#eq:kernel-polynomial-scale" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:kernel-polynomial-scale">[eq:kernel-polynomial-scale]</a>, the supremum and total variation of $F'/N$ on $[x_*,X]$ are $O(U^{-1})$. Partial summation therefore retains an $O(N^{-\nu})$ bound after including this weight. Use normalized Fourier coefficients on each finite period for the other weights. Since $\rho_D$ is a constant times one residue-class indicator, its Fourier $\ell^1$ norm is $n_{d_r}\le D$. For $B_u$ the bound by its period times its supremum gives Fourier $\ell^1$ norm at most $u^2$. Expanding the weights in <a href="#eq:prime-kernel" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:prime-kernel">[eq:prime-kernel]</a>, and using $\varphi(l)/l\le1$, yields $$\label{eq:prime-minor-kernel}
 |S_1(\alpha)|\ll D\sum_{u\le H}u^2N^{-\nu}
 \ll DH^3N^{-\nu}.$$ The powers in <a href="#eq:model-minor" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:model-minor">[eq:model-minor]</a> and <a href="#eq:prime-minor-kernel" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:prime-minor-kernel">[eq:prime-minor-kernel]</a> are $2\beta-\eta$ and $5\beta/2-\nu$, respectively. Both are smaller than $-\beta\gamma/4$, so these bounds are $O(H^{-\gamma/2})$.

<a id="eq:model-major"></a>
Now let $\alpha=\alpha_0+y$ be on a major arc, with $q=q(\alpha_0)\le N^\eta$ and $|y|\le3N^{-1+\eta}$. Decompose $\alpha_0=\alpha_D+\alpha_1$ into the prime-power denominator parts supported, respectively, on primes dividing $D$ and on primes not dividing $D$. The coefficient in <a href="#eq:model-transform" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:model-transform">[eq:model-transform]</a> belonging to the center $\alpha_0$, or zero if that center is absent, is <a id="eq:model-local-coefficient"></a>$$\label{eq:model-local-coefficient}
 c_0(\alpha_0)=
 \begin{cases}
 e(\alpha_Dr)\Gamma(\alpha_1),
       &q(\alpha_D)\mid D\ \text{and}\ \alpha_1\in\Xi,\\
 0,&\text{otherwise}.
 \end{cases}$$ Here the sign follows from $\alpha_D=-j'/D$ at that center. Every other center is at circle distance at least $1/(qDH)$ from $\alpha_0$. Since $|y|qDH=o(1)$, geometric summation in each of the $O(DH^2)$ terms gives $$\label{eq:model-major}
 S_0(\alpha)=c_0(\alpha_0)V_N(y)+O(qD^2H^3/N).$$

<a id="eq:weighted-prime-progression"></a>
We next derive the corresponding coefficient for $S_1$, including the normalization of the prime weights. For squarefree $u\le H$ coprime to $D$, let $A_u$ be the unit mean of $$b_u(m)=\rho_D(m)B_u(m)e(\alpha_0F(m)).$$ Its period divides $b=qDu$, and $\|b_u\|_\infty\le Du$. For $x_*\le x'<x''\le X$, Proposition <a href="#prop:prime-progressions" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:prime-progressions">14</a> gives $$\label{eq:weighted-prime-progression}
 \sum_{x'<m\le x''}b_u(m)\frac{\varphi(l)}l\Lambda'(T_l(m))
 =A_u(x''-x')+
 O\big(qD^2u^2Y^{31/32}\log^2(2lbY)\big).$$ Indeed, one class $m\equiv c\pmod b$ corresponds to the class $r_l+lc\pmod {lb}$ in a prime interval of length $l(x''-x')$. It is reduced exactly when $(T_l(c),b)=1$, since $(r_l,l)=1$. For such a class the factor $\varphi(l)/l$ changes the prime main term to $(x''-x')/n_b$, by <a href="#eq:unit-mean-count" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:unit-mean-count">[eq:unit-mean-count]</a>. On a nonreduced class the only possible primes divide $lb$, and their total logarithmic weight is $O(\log(lb))$, with zero main term. Apply the progression bound at both endpoints, then sum its errors over at most $b=qDu$ classes with weights of modulus at most $Du$. This proves <a href="#eq:weighted-prime-progression" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:weighted-prime-progression">[eq:weighted-prime-progression]</a>, including the nonreduced classes.

<a id="eq:prime-major"></a>
The smooth factor $F'(x)e(yF(x))/N$ has supremum plus total variation $$O\big((1+N|y|)/U\big).$$ For example, its derivative is bounded in integral by the contributions of $F''/N$ and $2\pi |y|(F')^2/N$, which are $O(U^{-1})$ and $O(N|y|/U)$ by <a href="#eq:kernel-polynomial-scale" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:kernel-polynomial-scale">[eq:kernel-polynomial-scale]</a>. Partial summation in <a href="#eq:weighted-prime-progression" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:weighted-prime-progression">[eq:weighted-prime-progression]</a> therefore produces main term $$\frac{A_u}{N}\int_{x_*}^{X}F'(x)e(yF(x))\,dx
 =\frac{A_u}{N}\int_{F(x_*)}^{N}e(yt')\,dt'.$$ The normalized integral $N^{-1}\int_{F(x_*)}^{N}e(yt')\,dt'$ differs from $V_N(y)$ by $O(a_l/N+(1+N|y|)/N)$: the missing initial interval has length $F(x_*)\ll a_l$, and comparison of the integral on $[0,N]$ with its integer sum costs $O(1+N|y|)$. It follows that $$\label{eq:prime-major}
 S_1(\alpha)=c(\alpha_0)V_N(y)+O(H^{-\gamma/2}),\qquad
 c(\alpha_0)=
 \sum_{\substack{u\le H\\u\text{ squarefree}\\(u,D)=1}}A_u.$$ Here are the error bounds underlying this assertion. Since $Y^{31/32}/U\ll lN^{-1/(32k)}$, the summed progression error is at most $$\frac{1+N|y|}{U}\,qD^2
 \sum_{u\le H}u^2Y^{31/32}N^{o(1)}
 \ll N^{2\eta+4\beta+\zeta-1/(32k)+o(1)}.$$ The replacement of the integrals costs, using $|A_u|\le Du$, $$O\big(DH^2(U^{-k}+N^{\eta-1})\big)
 \ll N^{2\beta+k\zeta-1}+N^{2\beta+\eta-1}.$$ The error in <a href="#eq:model-major" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:model-major">[eq:model-major]</a> is at most $N^{\eta+7\beta/2-1}$. All three estimates are $O(N^{-\beta\gamma/4})$: indeed $\beta,\zeta\le\eta/1000$, $\eta=1/(10000k)$, and $\gamma<1$. The first exponent has a strict margin, so its $o(1)$ loss is harmless.

<a id="eq:kernel-D-factor"></a>
It remains to compare $c$ and $c_0$. This is precisely the reason for the unit means and the regular argument classes used above. At primes dividing $D$, the common factor in each $A_u$ is $$\label{eq:kernel-D-factor}
 \chi_D(\alpha_0)=
 \begin{cases}
 e(\alpha_Dr),&q(\alpha_D)\mid D,\\
 0,&\text{otherwise}.
 \end{cases}$$ To see both cases, factor $\rho_D$ and the unit mean prime by prime. At $p\mid D$ the chosen class has weight of unit mean one and prescribed $F$-value modulo $p^{E_p}$. A character of conductor at most $p^{E_p}$ therefore gives its value at $r$. A primitive character of higher conductor averages to zero, since Lemma <a href="#lem:local-lifting" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:local-lifting">4</a> gives uniform higher lifts of $F$ on that class, all still satisfying the unit condition.

<a id="eq:kernel-local-realization"></a>
Put $q_1=q(\alpha_1)$. If $u$ has a prime factor outside $q_1$, its local $B_p$ factor has unit mean zero and $A_u=0$. For $p^j\Vert q_1$, write $\alpha_p$ for the $p^j$-part of $\alpha_1$ and let $$a_{p,j}=\mathbb E_{\substack{m\bmod p^j\\p\nmid T_l(m)}}
                  e(\alpha_pF(m)),\qquad
 b_{p,j}=\mathbb E_{\substack{m\bmod p^j\\p\nmid T_l(m)}}
                  B_p(m)e(\alpha_pF(m)).$$ The surviving $A_u$, for squarefree divisors $u$ of $q_1$, are $\chi_D(\alpha_0)$ times products that choose $b_{p,j}$ when $p\mid u$ and $a_{p,j}$ otherwise. Moreover, $$\label{eq:kernel-local-realization}
 a_{p,j}+b_{p,j}=
 \begin{cases}
 \Gamma_p(\alpha_p),&j=1,\\
 0,&j\ge2.
 \end{cases}$$ For $j=1$, the weight $1+B_p=\psi_p$ pushes the unit mean forward to $\pi_p$. For $j\ge2$, it is supported on allowed regular classes modulo $p$, and their uniform argument lifts give uniform lifts of the $F$-value. The primitive character therefore has mean zero.

If $q_1\le H$, every squarefree divisor needed in the product expansion occurs in the sum for $c$. Thus <a href="#eq:kernel-local-realization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:kernel-local-realization">[eq:kernel-local-realization]</a> and <a href="#eq:kernel-D-factor" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:kernel-D-factor">[eq:kernel-D-factor]</a> show that $c=c_0$, including the case of a nonsquarefree $q_1$, when both vanish. If $q_1>H$, the model coefficient is zero. Lemma <a href="#lem:local-sums" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:local-sums">3</a> gives $|a_{p,j}|\le p^{-8\delta j}$, while <a href="#eq:edge-multiplier-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:edge-multiplier-bound">[eq:edge-multiplier-bound]</a> and <a href="#eq:kernel-local-realization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:kernel-local-realization">[eq:kernel-local-realization]</a> give $|b_{p,j}|\le2p^{-\gamma j}$ (for $j\ge2$ one has $b_{p,j}=-a_{p,j}$). Bounding the truncated product expansion by the full sum of absolute values, and using <a href="#eq:prime-thresholds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:prime-thresholds">[eq:prime-thresholds]</a>, we obtain $$|c(\alpha_0)|\le
 \prod_{p^j\Vert q_1}(|a_{p,j}|+|b_{p,j}|)
 \le\prod_{p^j\Vert q_1}3p^{-\gamma j}
 \le q_1^{-\gamma/2}<H^{-\gamma/2}.$$ Indeed $p^{\gamma/2}\ge3$, so each factor is at most $p^{-\gamma j/2}$. Together with <a href="#eq:model-major" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:model-major">[eq:model-major]</a> and <a href="#eq:prime-major" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:prime-major">[eq:prime-major]</a>, this proves the comparison on major arcs. The minor-arc bounds already proved complete the lemma. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-kernel-comparison-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-kernel-comparison</summary>

*Proof of Proposition <a href="#prop:pair-cancellation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:pair-cancellation">18</a>.* Extend $J_1,J_2$ by zero to $\mathbb Z$ and put $$\mathcal B_i=\frac1N\sum_{x,n\in\mathbb Z}
                    k_i(n)J_1(x)J_2(x+n),\qquad i=0,1.$$ To keep track of signs and normalizations, let $\widetilde J_i(\alpha)=\sum_xJ_i(x)e(-\alpha x)$, with counting measure in this transform. Character orthogonality gives $$\mathcal B_i=\frac1N\int_{\mathbb R/\mathbb Z}
 S_i(\alpha)\widetilde J_1(-\alpha)
                 \widetilde J_2(\alpha)\,d\alpha.$$ Both counting-measure $\ell^2$ norms are at most $\sqrt N$. Cauchy–Schwarz, Plancherel, and Lemma <a href="#lem:kernel-comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:kernel-comparison">19</a> therefore imply $|\mathcal B_1-\mathcal B_0|\ll H^{-\gamma/2}$. The forbidden-support hypothesis gives $\mathcal B_1=0$. For every contributing pair in $\mathcal B_0$, the ordering and the residue classes imply $1\le n\le N$ and $n\equiv r\pmod D$. Substituting <a href="#eq:model-kernel" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:model-kernel">[eq:model-kernel]</a> and setting $y=x+n$ thus yields $$\mathcal B_0=\frac{D}{N^2}\sum_{\xi\in\Xi}\Gamma(\xi)
       \sum_{x,y}J_1(x)J_2(y)e(-\xi(y-x))
 =D\sum_{\xi\in\Xi}\Gamma(\xi)
       \widehat{J_1}_{[N]}(-\xi)\widehat{J_2}_{[N]}(\xi).$$ This is exactly <a href="#eq:pair-cancellation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:pair-cancellation">[eq:pair-cancellation]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:descent"></a>
### Descent to shorter intervals

We now combine the pair cancellation estimate with the seminorm furnished by the tuple laws. The quantity to be decreased is a diagonal tuple integral of a Fourier lift. Restriction to progressions compares this quantity with the same quantity on shorter intervals; avoidance makes a fixed proportion of the residue assignments cancel, apart from the small exceptional measures. This is the recurrence method of (OpenAI 2026a) <a id="pm-cite-37"></a>[[IntersectivePower](#ref-IntersectivePower)], adapted here to preserve the prime argument condition throughout the auxiliary family.

<a id="eq:descent-trivial"></a>
All structural parameters, including $M_0,d,\gamma$ and those in <a href="#eq:parameters" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:parameters">[eq:parameters]</a>, remain fixed. At every integer scale $N\ge1$ we use $\mathcal P_N,Q,H$ from <a href="#eq:kernel-cutoffs" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:kernel-cutoffs">[eq:kernel-cutoffs]</a>. For $l\ge1$ and $A\subseteq[N]$, define <a id="eq:descent-quantity"></a>$$\label{eq:descent-quantity}
 \begin{split}
 Y_l(N,A)&=Z_{l,\mathcal P_N}
       \bigl(\mathcal L_{[N],\mathcal P_N,Q}\mathbf1_A\bigr),\\
 \mathcal Y_l(N)&=\max_{\substack{A\subseteq[N]\\A\text{ avoiding at }l}}
                         Y_l(N,A).
 \end{split}$$ Here $Y_l$ is unrelated to the prime-sum range $Y$ used in the preceding section. Lemma <a href="#lem:seminorm" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:seminorm">9</a> and the constant Fourier coefficient of the lift give the first bound in $$\label{eq:descent-trivial}
 \left(\frac{|A|}{N}\right)^d\le Y_l(N,A),
 \qquad 0\le\mathcal Y_l(N)\ll N^{o(1)}.$$ For the upper bound, ordinary Hölder with the uniform single-slot marginals gives $Z_{l,\mathcal P_N}(g)\le\|g\|_d^d$. Apply Proposition <a href="#prop:lift-moments" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:lift-moments">11</a>; its length hypothesis holds for large $N$ since $6\beta<1$. For bounded $N$, bounding each term of the finite Fourier sum gives the same uniform conclusion. In particular, the upper bound in <a href="#eq:descent-trivial" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:descent-trivial">[eq:descent-trivial]</a> holds for every $l\ge1$, with no restriction of the form $l\le N^\zeta$.

<a id="eq:residue-average"></a>
For each $s\bmod M_0$ put $$f_s(x)=M_0\mathbf1_A(x)\mathbf1_{\{x\equiv s\pmod{M_0}\}},
 \qquad G_s=\mathcal L_{[N],\mathcal P_N,Q}f_s.$$ Averaging $f_s$ over uniform $s$ gives $\mathbf1_A$. Thus, for independent uniform residues $s_v\bmod M_0$ indexed by $v\in V$, multilinearity gives $$\label{eq:residue-average}
 Y_l(N,A)=\mathbb E_{(s_v)_{v\in V}}
          \mathcal Z_{l,\mathcal P_N}((G_{s_v})_{v\in V}).$$ We shall bound these integrals in two ways. The comparison with shorter intervals holds for every $l$; cancellation will require the analytic range $l\le N^\zeta$.

<a id="pm-h-28"></a>
#### Specialization and progression coordinates

Fixing prime coordinates of a lift restricts its underlying set to a residue class, provided the remaining Fourier denominator is sufficiently small. The following exact formula records the normalization.

<a id="stmt-lem-residue-specialization"></a>
<div id="lem:residue-specialization" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:residue-specialization"></a>
**Lemma 20** (Residue specialization). *Let $N\ge1$, $A\subseteq[N]$, $s\bmod M_0$, and let $I\subseteq[N]$ be a nonempty consecutive interval. Let $B\subseteq\mathcal P_N$, write $q_B=\prod_{p\in B}p$, $D=M_0q_B$, and $\mathcal S=\mathcal P_N\setminus B$. Fix the coordinates indexed by $B$ of $\mathcal L_{I,\mathcal P_N,Q}f_s$ at $b=(b_p)_{p\in B}$, obtaining $g_I:X_{\mathcal S}\to\mathbb R$. Let $a'\bmod D$ be the unique residue satisfying $a'\equiv s\pmod{M_0}$ and $a'\equiv b_p\pmod p$ for $p\in B$. Then every frequency $\xi$ on $X_{\mathcal S}$ with $q(\xi)q_B\le Q$ satisfies $$\label{eq:residue-specialization}
 \widehat{g_I}(\xi)=\frac{D}{|I|}
       \sum_{\substack{x\in I\\x\equiv a'\pmod D}}
                       \mathbf1_A(x)e(-x\xi).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-residue-specialization"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-residue-specialization</summary>

*Proof.* The coefficient on the left is the sum of $\widehat{f_s}_I(\xi+\omega)e(b\omega)$ over the frequencies $\omega$ on $X_B$ retained by the lift. The denominator condition retains every $\omega$, because $q(\xi+\omega)=q(\xi)q(\omega)\le Q$. Orthogonality of these $q_B$ characters imposes $x\equiv b_p\pmod p$ for every $p\in B$ and contributes the factor $q_B$. Together with the factor $M_0$ in $f_s$, this gives <a href="#eq:residue-specialization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:residue-specialization">[eq:residue-specialization]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-prop-shorter-scale"></a>
<div id="prop:shorter-scale" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:shorter-scale"></a>
<a id="eq:shorter-scale-size"></a>
**Proposition 21** (Comparison with shorter intervals). *For all sufficiently large $N$, every $l\ge1$, and every set $A\subseteq[N]$ avoiding at $l$, the following holds. Let $B\subseteq\mathcal P_N$ satisfy $q_B\le N^\tau$, and put $$D=M_0q_B,\qquad L_D=\lambda(D),\qquad
 n_B=\left\lceil\frac{N}{L_D}\right\rceil,\qquad
 \Lambda_B=\frac{L_Dn_B}{N},\qquad
 \mathcal S=\mathcal P_N\setminus B.$$ For each $v\in V$, choose $s_v\bmod M_0$ and fix the $B$-coordinates of $G_{s_v}$ arbitrarily, obtaining $g_v:X_{\mathcal S}\to\mathbb R$. Then $$\label{eq:shorter-scale}
 \bigl|\mathcal Z_{l,\mathcal S}((g_v)_{v\in V})\bigr|
 \le\Lambda_B^d\mathcal Y_{lD}(n_B)+O(N^{-\sigma}),$$ uniformly in all these choices. The new scale and normalization satisfy $$\label{eq:shorter-scale-size}
 N^{1/2}\le M_0^{-k}N^{1-k\tau}\le n_B<N,
 \qquad 1\le\Lambda_B\le1+M_0^kN^{k\tau-1}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-shorter-scale"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-shorter-scale</summary>

*Proof.* The scale bounds follow from $D\le L_D\le D^k$ and $M_0\ge2$; their first inequality holds for sufficiently large $N$ because $k\tau<1/2$. In particular, with $Q'=n_B^\beta$, <a id="eq:shorter-cutoffs"></a>$$\label{eq:shorter-cutoffs}
 H\le Q'\le Q.$$ By Proposition <a href="#prop:signed-holder" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:signed-holder">8</a>, it is enough to prove <a href="#eq:shorter-scale" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:shorter-scale">[eq:shorter-scale]</a> for $Z_{l,\mathcal S}(g)$, where $g$ is any one of the specialized inputs.

<a id="eq:specialization-tail"></a>
First keep only the frequencies of $g$ with denominator at most $H$, and call the resulting real function $g_{\le H}$. Telescope the diagonal integral one slot at a time. Lemma <a href="#lem:large-denominator" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:large-denominator">10</a>, followed by Proposition <a href="#prop:lift-moments" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:lift-moments">11</a> and Lemma <a href="#lem:lift-specialization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:lift-specialization">12</a>, bounds the change by $$\label{eq:specialization-tail}
 \bigl|Z_{l,\mathcal S}(g)-Z_{l,\mathcal S}(g_{\le H})\bigr|
 \ll H^{-\gamma}q_BN^{o(1)}=O(N^{-\sigma}).$$ Indeed specialization costs $q_B^{1/2}$ in the $L^2$ norm of the removed part, and $q_B^{1/(2(d-1))}$ in each of the other $d-1$ norms. Their product is $q_B$. All frequency selections retain whole exact supports before specialization, as required by the moment bound. Here $N\ge10Q^6$ for large $N$, and the saving follows from $\beta\gamma/2-\tau>\sigma$.

The auxiliary-family identity transports avoidance along progressions of step $L_D=\lambda(D)$. Since $D\mid L_D$, we split the prescribed class modulo $D$ into its $L_D/D$ classes modulo $L_D$. Let $a'\bmod D$ be the class determined by this specialization. For $j\in[L_D]$ with $j\equiv a'\pmod D$, set $$c_j=j-L_D,\qquad
 A'_j=\{x'\in[n_B]:L_Dx'+c_j\in A\},\qquad
 \mathcal H_j=\mathcal L_{[n_B],\mathcal S,H}\mathbf1_{A'_j}.$$ Every integer of $A$ in the class $a'\bmod D$ has a unique representation $L_Dx'+c_j$ of this form: take $j$ to be its representative in $[L_D]$. The definition by membership in $A$ excludes any surplus points beyond $N$ in these progressions.

Each $A'_j$ avoids at $lD$. More explicitly, a nonzero difference $h_{lD}(m)$ with $m\ge1$ and $T_{lD}(m)$ prime would give the difference $\lambda(D)h_{lD}(m)=h_l(Dm-i)$ in $A$, where $0\le i<D$ is supplied by <a href="#eq:auxiliary-change" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:auxiliary-change">[eq:auxiliary-change]</a>. We have $Dm-i\ge1$ and $T_l(Dm-i)=T_{lD}(m)$, so this would violate avoidance at $l$. The nonzero condition is preserved because $\lambda(D)>0$.

<a id="eq:progression-fibers"></a>
Since $q_BH\le Q$, Lemma <a href="#lem:residue-specialization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:residue-specialization">20</a> now gives the exact identity $$\label{eq:progression-fibers}
 g_{\le H}(z)=\frac{\Lambda_B}{L_D/D}
       \sum_{\substack{j\in[L_D]\\j\equiv a'\pmod D}}
       \mathcal H_j\bigl(L_D^{-1}(z-c_j)\bigr).$$ To check it, every prime divisor of $L_D$ divides $D$, so multiplication by $L_D$ is invertible on $X_{\mathcal S}$ and preserves Fourier denominators. For $q(\xi)\le H$, the coefficient of the $j$th summand at $\xi$ is $\widehat{\mathcal H_j}(L_D\xi)e(-c_j\xi)$. Multiplication by the prefactor $\Lambda_B/(L_D/D)=Dn_B/N$ and summation over $j$ therefore gives $(D/N)\sum_{x\in A,\ x\equiv a'\ (D)}e(-x\xi)$, which is exactly the coefficient in <a href="#eq:residue-specialization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:residue-specialization">[eq:residue-specialization]</a> for $I=[N]$. Both sides of <a href="#eq:progression-fibers" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:progression-fibers">[eq:progression-fibers]</a> have zero coefficients at denominators greater than $H$.

<a id="eq:fiber-seminorm"></a>
There are $L_D/D$ summands. The triangle inequality from Lemma <a href="#lem:seminorm" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:seminorm">9</a> and its transport identity <a href="#eq:lift-transport" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lift-transport">[eq:lift-transport]</a> imply $$\label{eq:fiber-seminorm}
 Z_{l,\mathcal S}(g_{\le H})
       \le\Lambda_B^d\max_j Z_{lD,\mathcal S}(\mathcal H_j).$$ Increase the cutoff in $\mathcal H_j$ from $H$ to $Q'$. The same large-denominator argument, now without specialization, changes its diagonal integral by $O(H^{-\gamma}N^{o(1)})=O(N^{-\sigma})$. The moment hypothesis is valid at the new scale: $n_B\ge10(Q')^6$ for large $N$, by $6\beta<1$ and <a href="#eq:shorter-scale-size" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:shorter-scale-size">[eq:shorter-scale-size]</a>.

The extended lift is obtained from $\mathcal L_{[n_B],\mathcal P_{n_B},Q'}\mathbf1_{A'_j}$ by averaging out its coordinates in $B\cap\mathcal P_{n_B}$ and adding unused coordinates in $\mathcal S\setminus\mathcal P_{n_B}$. Averaging contracts the seminorm, and adding unused coordinates does not change it, by Lemma <a href="#lem:seminorm" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:seminorm">9</a>. Consequently its diagonal integral is at most $Y_{lD}(n_B,A'_j)\le\mathcal Y_{lD}(n_B)$. Combining this observation with <a href="#eq:specialization-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:specialization-tail">[eq:specialization-tail]</a> and <a href="#eq:fiber-seminorm" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:fiber-seminorm">[eq:fiber-seminorm]</a> proves the diagonal bound, hence the stated multilinear bound. None of these steps restricts the size of $l$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-29"></a>
#### Cancellation for admissible residue assignments

<a id="eq:good-proportion"></a>
The preceding proposition controls every assignment in <a href="#eq:residue-average" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:residue-average">[eq:residue-average]</a>. We now obtain a stronger bound for those assignments whose cycle increments can occur as local polynomial values. Call $(s_v)_{v\in V}$ *good at $l$* if every $s_{v_{j+1}}-s_{v_j}$, $0\le j<L$, is admissible modulo $M_0$ at $l$. Let $\rho_l$ be the proportion of good assignments. The zero-sum admissible sequences of Lemma <a href="#lem:admissible-cycle" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:admissible-cycle">5</a> give $$\label{eq:good-proportion}
 \rho_l\ge\rho_*:=M_0^{1-L}>0.$$ Indeed combine such a sequence at each prime power dividing $M_0$ by the Chinese remainder theorem. After prescribing these $L$ increments, the initial cycle residue and every residue off the cycle are free.

<a id="stmt-prop-descent-cancellation"></a>
<div id="prop:descent-cancellation" class="proposition">

**Proposition 22** (Cancellation on the nonexceptional laws). *For all sufficiently large $N$, all $1\le l\le N^\zeta$, every $A\subseteq[N]$ avoiding at $l$, and every good assignment $(s_v)_{v\in V}$, <a id="eq:descent-cancellation"></a>$$\label{eq:descent-cancellation}
 \left|\int\prod_{v\in V}G_{s_v}(z_v)\,
       d\bigotimes_{p\in\mathcal P_N}(\mu_{l,p}-\varepsilon_{l,p})\right|
       \ll N^{-\sigma}.$$ The implied constant is uniform in $l$, $A$, and the assignment.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-descent-cancellation"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-descent-cancellation</summary>

*Proof.* The aim is to leave just the two endpoint lifts on an ordered cycle edge. We first average over short intervals, then truncate and expand the other inputs. Each expansion term uses prime coordinates with small product; conditioning on those coordinates leaves the pair to which Proposition <a href="#prop:pair-cancellation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:pair-cancellation">18</a> applies.

It suffices to use the probability laws $\mu_{l,p}^\circ$, since restoring the omitted masses multiplies the integral by $\prod_p(1-\eta_{l,p})\le1$. Partition $[N]$ into $m=\lfloor N^\tau\rfloor$ consecutive intervals of lengths as equal as possible. Each $G_s$ is the weighted average of $\mathcal L_{I,\mathcal P_N,Q}f_s$ over these intervals, with weights $|I|/N$. Thus the integral is an average over independently chosen intervals for all slots. Their lengths are comparable to $N^{1-\tau}$, so the lift moment bound applies because $6\beta+\tau<1$. Ordinary Hölder and uniform marginals bound every interval assignment by $N^{o(1)}$ in absolute value.

The probability that all $L$ cycle slots use the same interval is $\sum_I(|I|/N)^L=O(N^{-(L-1)\tau})$. These assignments contribute $O(N^{-\sigma})$. For any remaining assignment, the cyclic sequence of interval indices is nonconstant, and hence has a strict increase along some directed cycle edge. Choose such an edge, with first endpoint in $I_1$ and second endpoint in the later interval $I_2$.

Keep the two endpoint lifts unchanged and truncate every other lift to denominators at most $T=N^\tau$. Telescoping with Lemma <a href="#lem:large-denominator" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:large-denominator">10</a> and the lift moment bound costs $O(T^{-\gamma}N^{o(1)})=O(N^{-\sigma})$. Expand the truncated $d-2$ inputs into characters. Their coefficients have absolute value at most $M_0$, and each input has $O(T^2)$ frequencies, so the sum of absolute coefficients in this product expansion is $O(T^{2(d-2)})$.

<a id="eq:conditioned-pair"></a>
<a id="eq:conditioned-pair-tail"></a>
Fix one term. Let $B$ be the union of the prime supports of its characters and write $$R=q_B\le T^{d-2},\qquad D=M_0R,\qquad
 \mathcal S=\mathcal P_N\setminus B.$$ Condition on the entire tuple of labels at every prime in $B$, taking any conditioning with positive probability under the product of the $\mu_{l,p}^\circ$. The expanded characters become constants of modulus one. The specialized endpoint lifts give functions $g_1,g_2$ on $X_{\mathcal S}$, and the laws at primes in $\mathcal S$ remain unchanged. Translation invariance shows that their pair integral is $$\label{eq:conditioned-pair}
 \sum_{\xi\text{ on }X_{\mathcal S}}
       \widehat g_1(-\xi)\widehat g_2(\xi)\Gamma(\xi),$$ where $\Gamma$ is the multiplier for this directed cycle edge. Indeed only opposite characters survive common translation, and their product is the character of the second label minus the first. By <a href="#eq:edge-multiplier-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:edge-multiplier-bound">[eq:edge-multiplier-bound]</a>, Parseval, and the specialized moment bound, the part with $q(\xi)>H$ is at most $$\label{eq:conditioned-pair-tail}
 H^{-\gamma}\|g_1\|_2\|g_2\|_2
       \ll H^{-\gamma}R N^{o(1)}.$$

<a id="eq:conditioned-pair-main"></a>
For the remaining frequencies, $HR\le Q$ and $D\le N^\beta$ for large $N$, since $(d-2)\tau<\beta/2$. Apply Lemma <a href="#lem:residue-specialization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:residue-specialization">20</a> to the endpoint lifts, obtaining classes $a'_1,a'_2\bmod D$. Their directed difference is admissible at $l$: goodness supplies admissibility modulo $M_0$, and the cycle property of $\mu_{l,p}^\circ$ supplies it at the conditioned primes $p\in B$. Set $$J_i=\mathbf1_A\mathbf1_{I_i}\mathbf1_{\{x\equiv a'_i\pmod D\}},
 \qquad i=1,2,$$ as functions on $[N]$. Every contributing pair $x,y$ has $x<y$ because $I_1$ precedes $I_2$. Avoidance therefore gives $y-x\notin\mathcal D_l$. All hypotheses of Proposition <a href="#prop:pair-cancellation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:pair-cancellation">18</a> hold. Its frequency set $\Xi$ is exactly the remaining frequencies on $X_{\mathcal S}$ with denominator at most $H$: coprimality with $D$ excludes the primes below $P$ and the primes in $B$, while every prime in a denominator at most $H$ is at most $Q$. The coefficient identity becomes $$\widehat g_i(\xi)=\frac{DN}{|I_i|}\widehat{J_i}_{[N]}(\xi)
 \qquad(q(\xi)\le H).$$ Consequently <a href="#eq:pair-cancellation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:pair-cancellation">[eq:pair-cancellation]</a> bounds the low-denominator part of <a href="#eq:conditioned-pair" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:conditioned-pair">[eq:conditioned-pair]</a> by $$\label{eq:conditioned-pair-main}
 O\left(H^{-\gamma/2}D\frac{N^2}{|I_1||I_2|}\right)
       =O(H^{-\gamma/2}DN^{2\tau}).$$

After multiplication by the expansion bound $O(T^{2(d-2)})$, both <a href="#eq:conditioned-pair-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:conditioned-pair-tail">[eq:conditioned-pair-tail]</a> and <a href="#eq:conditioned-pair-main" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:conditioned-pair-main">[eq:conditioned-pair-main]</a> are bounded by $$N^{-\beta\gamma/4+(3d+2)\tau+o(1)}=O(N^{-\sigma}),$$ since <a href="#eq:parameters" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:parameters">[eq:parameters]</a> gives $\beta\gamma/4>(3d+2)\tau+\sigma$. These estimates are uniform in the conditioning, in the selected edge, and in the interval assignment. Averaging, and including the earlier truncation and coincident-interval errors, proves the proposition. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-30"></a>
#### The contracting recurrence

<a id="eq:exceptional-budget"></a>
We have obtained cancellation after removing the exceptional part of every local law. To return to the full laws in <a href="#eq:residue-average" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:residue-average">[eq:residue-average]</a>, we expand that removal. The total mass of the resulting exceptional terms is small enough to pay for their possibly shorter progression scales. For $B\subseteq\mathcal P_N$ write $$\eta_{l,B}=\prod_{p\in B}\eta_{l,p},\qquad
 D_B=M_0q_B,\qquad
 n_B=\left\lceil\frac{N}{\lambda(D_B)}\right\rceil.$$ Thus $D_\varnothing=M_0$. The mass estimate in Proposition <a href="#prop:tuple-laws" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:tuple-laws">6</a> and the fixed choice of $P$ give, uniformly in $l$, $$\label{eq:exceptional-budget}
 \sum_{\varnothing\ne B\subseteq\mathcal P_N}q_B\eta_{l,B}
 \le\prod_{p\ge P}(1+p^{-3})-1\le\frac14.$$

<a id="stmt-prop-descent-recurrence"></a>
<div id="prop:descent-recurrence" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:descent-recurrence"></a>
**Proposition 23** (Recurrence). *There is a constant $C_1$ such that for every sufficiently large $N$ and $1\le l\le N^\zeta$, $$\label{eq:descent-recurrence}
 \begin{split}
 \mathcal Y_l(N)\le {}&C_1N^{-\sigma}+(1+u_N)\biggl[
 (1-\rho_l)\mathcal Y_{lM_0}(n_\varnothing)\\
 &\hspace{12mm}+\rho_l
 \sum_{\substack{\varnothing\ne B\subseteq\mathcal P_N\\q_B\le N^\tau}}
       \eta_{l,B}\mathcal Y_{lD_B}(n_B)\biggr],\\
 u_N={}&(1+M_0^kN^{k\tau-1})^d-1=o(1).
 \end{split}$$ The constant and the sufficiently-large threshold are independent of $l$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-descent-recurrence"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-descent-recurrence</summary>

*Proof.* Fix an avoiding set $A$. For assignments in <a href="#eq:residue-average" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:residue-average">[eq:residue-average]</a> that are not good, apply Proposition <a href="#prop:shorter-scale" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:shorter-scale">21</a> with $B=\varnothing$. Their proportion is $1-\rho_l$. For a good assignment, Proposition <a href="#prop:descent-cancellation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:descent-cancellation">22</a> handles the product of the measures $\mu_{l,p}-\varepsilon_{l,p}$. The difference from the full product is $$\bigotimes_{p\in\mathcal P_N}\mu_{l,p}
 -\bigotimes_{p\in\mathcal P_N}(\mu_{l,p}-\varepsilon_{l,p})
 =\sum_{\varnothing\ne B\subseteq\mathcal P_N}(-1)^{|B|+1}
   \left(\bigotimes_{p\in B}\varepsilon_{l,p}\right)
   \otimes\left(\bigotimes_{p\notin B}\mu_{l,p}\right).$$ The positive product measure in the $B$th summand has mass $\eta_{l,B}$. If this mass is zero the term vanishes. Otherwise, after normalization, every single-slot marginal is uniform by simultaneous translation invariance. Hölder and Proposition <a href="#prop:lift-moments" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:lift-moments">11</a> thus bound its integral by $N^{o(1)}$ in absolute value. The terms with $q_B>N^\tau$ have total mass at most $N^{-\tau}/4$ by <a href="#eq:exceptional-budget" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:exceptional-budget">[eq:exceptional-budget]</a>, and hence contribute $O(N^{-\sigma})$.

For $q_B\le N^\tau$, condition on all labels at the primes in $B$. The remaining law is the product of the full measures $\mu_{l,p}$, so Proposition <a href="#prop:shorter-scale" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:shorter-scale">21</a> applies to the specialized inputs. Its factor $\Lambda_B^d$ is at most $1+u_N$ by <a href="#eq:shorter-scale-size" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:shorter-scale-size">[eq:shorter-scale-size]</a>, and its errors sum to $O(N^{-\sigma})$ by <a href="#eq:exceptional-budget" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:exceptional-budget">[eq:exceptional-budget]</a>. Taking absolute upper bounds in the signed expansion and averaging over the good assignments gives their contribution in <a href="#eq:descent-recurrence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:descent-recurrence">[eq:descent-recurrence]</a>. Finally maximize over $A$ in <a href="#eq:residue-average" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:residue-average">[eq:residue-average]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-31"></a>
#### Uniform induction and the density bound

The recurrence uses prime estimates only when $l\le N^\zeta$, but its successors $lD_B$ need not lie in the corresponding analytic range at $n_B$. We therefore prove a bound simultaneously for every auxiliary parameter $l$. The factor $l^{b_0}$ below covers the complementary range by the elementary bound <a href="#eq:descent-trivial" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:descent-trivial">[eq:descent-trivial]</a>.

<a id="stmt-prop-uniform-descent"></a>
<div id="prop:uniform-descent" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:uniform-descent"></a>
**Proposition 24** (Uniform power decay). *There are constants $a_0,b_0>0$ and $C_0\ge1$, depending only on $h$, such that for all integers $l,N\ge1$, $$\label{eq:uniform-descent}
 \mathcal Y_l(N)\le C_0l^{b_0}N^{-a_0}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-uniform-descent"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-uniform-descent</summary>

<a id="eq:induction-parameters"></a>
*Proof.* After all parameters in <a href="#eq:parameters" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:parameters">[eq:parameters]</a> have been fixed, choose $a_0>0$ sufficiently small and put $b_0=2a_0/\zeta$, so that $$\label{eq:induction-parameters}
 a_0<\sigma,\qquad s_0:=ka_0+b_0\le1,\qquad
 \theta_0:=M_0^{s_0}(1-3\rho_*/4)<1.$$ This is possible because $s_0\to0$ with $a_0$ while $\rho_*>0$ is fixed. If $l>N^\zeta$, then $$l^{b_0}N^{-a_0}\ge N^{a_0}.$$ The estimate <a href="#eq:descent-trivial" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:descent-trivial">[eq:descent-trivial]</a>, used with exponent $a_0$, proves <a href="#eq:uniform-descent" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:uniform-descent">[eq:uniform-descent]</a> in this range after a fixed enlargement of $C_0$. This treats all such $l,N$ before the induction.

For the remaining pairs, induct on the integer $N$, simultaneously for all $l\ge1$. At a sufficiently large scale with $l\le N^\zeta$, every successor in <a href="#eq:descent-recurrence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:descent-recurrence">[eq:descent-recurrence]</a> satisfies $n_B<N$ by <a href="#eq:shorter-scale-size" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:shorter-scale-size">[eq:shorter-scale-size]</a>. The induction hypothesis is available at $lD_B$ whether or not that parameter satisfies $lD_B\le n_B^\zeta$. Since $n_B\ge N/D_B^k$, it gives $$\mathcal Y_{lD_B}(n_B)
 \le C_0(lD_B)^{b_0}n_B^{-a_0}
 \le C_0l^{b_0}N^{-a_0}D_B^{s_0}.$$ Use $q_B^{s_0}\le q_B$ and <a href="#eq:exceptional-budget" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:exceptional-budget">[eq:exceptional-budget]</a> to bound the bracket in <a href="#eq:descent-recurrence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:descent-recurrence">[eq:descent-recurrence]</a> by $$\begin{split}
 C_0l^{b_0}N^{-a_0}M_0^{s_0}
   \bigl(1-\rho_l+\rho_l/4\bigr)
 &\le C_0l^{b_0}N^{-a_0}\theta_0,
 \end{split}$$ where <a href="#eq:good-proportion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:good-proportion">[eq:good-proportion]</a> was used in the last step. Choose a fixed threshold $N_*$, large enough for the recurrence and for $$C_1N^{a_0-\sigma}+(1+u_N)\theta_0\le1
 \qquad(N\ge N_*).$$ Such a threshold exists by <a href="#eq:induction-parameters" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:induction-parameters">[eq:induction-parameters]</a> and $u_N=o(1)$; it is independent of $C_0\ge1$. Dividing the recurrence by $C_0l^{b_0}N^{-a_0}$ now proves the induction step, since $C_0l^{b_0}\ge1$. Finally choose $C_0$ large enough to cover both the previously treated range $l>N^\zeta$ and every $N<N_*$, uniformly in $l$, by <a href="#eq:descent-trivial" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:descent-trivial">[eq:descent-trivial]</a>. This completes the induction. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-uniform-descent-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-uniform-descent</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>.* At $l=1$ we have $T_1(m)=m$ and $h_1=h$, so the hypothesis of the theorem is exactly avoidance at $l=1$. Combining <a href="#eq:descent-trivial" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:descent-trivial">[eq:descent-trivial]</a> with <a href="#eq:uniform-descent" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:uniform-descent">[eq:uniform-descent]</a> gives $$\left(\frac{|A|}{N}\right)^d\le C_0N^{-a_0},
 \qquad |A|\le C_0^{1/d}N^{1-a_0/d}.$$ Thus one may take $c_h=a_0/d$ and $C_h=C_0^{1/d}$, for every $N\ge1$. Only strictly ordered pairs, and hence positive differences, were used in cancellation. Restriction to progressions preserved nonzero forbidden values and their prime arguments. A value $h(p)=0$ therefore imposes no additional condition at any stage. ◻

</details>

  "figures": [
    {
      "caption": "Marking the cut block $b$: the comparison between $S$ and $S′ = S ∪ {b}$. For each new address $u$, concatenate its $r_{*}$ old plus lists; the displayed sublists are schematic. Independence and probabilities refer to sampling before weights are applied. Restricting the new index at each plus slot to its former sublist recovers the old distribution of selected labels, with probability $r_{*}^{−d/2}$. Every edge between new addresses gives an old edge between every pair of their plus sublists. Hence every factor of $I_{S′}$ occurs in $I_{+}$, and $0 ≤ W ≤ 1$ gives $I_{S′} ≥ I_{+}$. These are the two ingredients of the comparison for $Q_{S′}(J)$.",
      "figure_id": "fig:list-concatenation",
      "pdf_page": 13,
      "source_evidence": [

          "source_object": "fig:0001"
        }
      ],

{$(u,\\sigma_a)$};\n    \\node[anchor=south] at (9.2,.42) {$(u,\\sigma_{r_*})$};\n    \\node[oldlist] (first) at (1.6,0)\n      {$(Y_{(u,\\sigma_1),i})_{i\\in[\\ell_s]}$};\n    \\node[oldlist,chosen] (middle) at (5.4,0)\n      {$(Y_{(u,\\sigma_a),i})_{i\\in[\\ell_s]}$};\n    \\node[oldlist] (last) at (9.2,0)\n      {$(Y_{(u,\\sigma_{r_*}),i})_{i\\in[\\ell_s]}$};\n    \\node at (3.5,0) {$\\cdots$};\n    \\node at (7.3,0) {$\\cdots$};\n    \\draw[guide] (first.south) -- (1.6,-1.18);\n    \\draw[guide] (middle.south) -- (5.4,-1.18);\n    \\draw[guide] (last.south) -- (9.2,-1.18);\n    \\node[fill=white,inner sep=2pt] at (5.4,-.8) {concatenate};\n\n    \\fill[blue!7] (3.8,-1.2) rectangle (7,-2);\n    \\draw[black!65] (0,-1.2) rectangle (10.8,-2);\n    \\draw[black!65] (3.2,-1.2) --       "witness_description": "Schematic diagram showing the concatenation of old plus lists into a new list $\\widetilde{Y}_u$. Vertical lines lead down through an arrow labeled \"concatenate\" into a partitioned horizontal box divided into sections labeled \"sublist 1\", \"$\\cdots$\", \"sublist $a$\", \"$\\cdots$\", \"sublist $r_*$\".
